--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
The mission is AGGREGATION - the score increases by 1 for every robot outside the target region (the BLACK-floored strip bordered by BLUE walls). So robots need to get to the BLACK strip with BLUE walls and stay there.

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor color: BLACK = target strip (or other black strip), GRAY = middle area
- Wall colors: BLUE = target border, GREEN = wrong border
- Neighbours: useful for aggregation behavior

**Wait - re-reading:** The score increases for robots OUTSIDE the target region. The target region is the strip with BLUE-bordered walls and BLACK floor. So robots need to find the BLACK floor area that is adjacent to BLUE walls.

**Strategy:**
- Robots need to move toward BLUE walls (GoToColor with blue = color 3)
- Once on BLACK floor, they should stay (stop or attract to neighbors)
- GoAwayColor with GREEN (color 2) to avoid wrong strip

**Champion analysis:** The champion uses 3 states of Exploration with various transitions. Score 16918.68 out of max 20*1200=24000. Not great - let's try more directed approaches.

**Plan:**
1. Elitism: Copy champion exactly
2. Exploitation: Small edits to champion
3. Exploration: Use GoToColor (B=8) toward blue walls, use floor-color transitions to detect black strips

For GoToColor blue: clr=3 (blue), for green: clr=2
Colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

Let me design better machines:
- State 0: GoToColor blue (move toward blue walls) 
- State 1: Stop or Attraction when on black floor
- Transition: if floor is BLACK → stop/aggregate

FSM designs:

1. **Champion (exact copy)**
2. **Champion variant 1**: adjust probabilities slightly
3. **Champion variant 2**: adjust thresholds slightly
4. **2-state: GoToBlue + Stop on black**
5. **3-state: GoToBlue, Attract on black, GoAway green**
6. **2-state: Exploration + Attraction**
7. **3-state directed navigation**
8. **GoToBlue primary**
9. **Spiral to explore + GoToBlue**
10. **Attraction-based aggregation**
11. **GoAwayGreen + Stop**
12. **Mixed approach**
13. **FlockCenter approach**
14. **ZigZag exploration**

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:** Score 5108.27. The champion uses:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=50), transitions to State 1 on BLACK floor (p=0.8) or randomly (p=0.1)
- State 1: Attraction (att=3), transitions back to State 0 on GRAY floor (p=0.9) or few neighbors (p=1, thresh=1) or BLACK floor (p=0.1)
- State 2: GoToColor blue (clr=3), transitions to State 1 on BLACK floor (p=0.9) or randomly (p=0.05)

Wait - State 2 seems to be GoToBlue but it doesn't connect from State 0. Actually State 0 has only 2 transitions (to State 1), so State 2 is never reached from State 0 directly. State 1 transitions to States 0 and 1 (itself not allowed) - actually n1x1 D=1 means other state index 1, which with 3 states leaving state 1, others are [0,2], so D=1 = state 2. So state 1 transitions to state 2 when few neighbors.

This makes sense: explore → when on black, attract → if neighbors thin out, go to blue wall → when on black again, attract.

**Strategy for improvements:**
- Keep this topology but tune parameters
- Try GoToBlue as primary state with attraction on black
- Add more transitions mixing floor color and neighbor count

**LED colors:** Only 0, 4, 5, 6 allowed (not 1, 2, 3).

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (Score: 3757.34):**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=50) → State 1 on BLACK floor (p=0.8), State 1 when sees BLUE color (p=0.85), State 1 randomly (p=0.1)
- State 1: Attraction (att=2) → State 0 on GRAY (p=0.9), State 2 when few neighbors (thresh=2, w=3), State 0 on BLACK (p=0.1)
- State 2: GoToColor blue → State 1 on BLACK (p=0.9), State 0 randomly (p=0.05)

Key improvements from round 4→5: Adding the blue-color perception trigger to state 0 made a big difference (3757 vs 4300). The robot now directly goes to attract when it sees blue walls.

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Tune the blue-perception probability (try 0.9, 0.95, 0.8)

3. Tune neighbor threshold (thresh=1, 3, w=2, 4, 5)
4. Tune random escape probabilities
5. Add blue-color perception to state 2 as well
6. Try higher rwm for more exploration coverage

**Exploration ideas:**
- State 1 also perceives blue to go to state 2 directly
- Add a 4th state for GoAwayGreen
- Try FlockCenter in state 1

## Reasoning

The champion hasn't improved in round 6, staying at 3757.34. The champion's structure is solid:
- State 0: Explore → go to attract (state 1) when: BLACK floor (p=0.8), sees blue color (p=0.85), or random (p=0.1)
- State 1: Attract (att=2) → go to explore (state 0) on GRAY (p=0.9), go to GoToBlue (state 2) when few neighbors (thresh=2, w=3), go to explore on BLACK (p=0.1)
- State 2: GoToBlue → state 1 on BLACK (p=0.9), state 0 randomly (p=0.05)

The champion's "see blue → attract" shortcut is key. Let me focus exploitation on:
1. Tuning the blue-color perception probability more finely (0.8, 0.88, 0.92)
2. Tuning w1x1 steepness (2, 4, 5, 6)
3. Tuning p1x0 (0.85, 0.95)
4. Adding 4th transition to state 1 (see blue → go to state 2)
5. Making state 2 also respond to seeing blue color to stay in GoToBlue
6. Different att values (1.5, 2.5)

For exploration: try making GoToBlue the primary state with attraction as secondary.

## Reasoning

The champion has been stuck at 3757.34 for three rounds now. The structure is clearly good but we're stuck in a local optimum. Time for more aggressive exploration while keeping exploitation of the champion.

**Current champion structure:**
- S0: Explore → S1 on BLACK(0.8), S1 on blue-visible(0.85), S1 random(0.1)
- S1: Attract(2) → S0 on GRAY(0.9), S2 on few-neighbors(thresh=2,w=3), S0 on BLACK(0.1)
- S2: GoToBlue → S1 on BLACK(0.9), S0 random(0.05)

**Key observations:**
- The "see blue → attract" shortcut in S0 is crucial
- att=2 is better than higher values
- The GoToBlue recovery state (S2) is important

**New ideas to try:**
1. Make S1 also transition to S2 when it sees blue (instead of when neighbors thin out)
2. Try Stop instead of Attraction (robots on black should just stop)
3. Try making the neighbor threshold condition use above-threshold to STAY (C=3 to loop) 
4. Try w=2 for steeper sigmoid (more decisive)
5. Try having S2 also go to S1 when it sees many neighbors (already found them)
6. Try FlockCenter with lower gain
7. Add transition from S0 directly to S2 (GoToBlue) when NOT seeing blue
8. Try 4-state with dedicated "stop on black" state

## Reasoning

This is the final round. The champion has been stuck at 3459.22 for two rounds. Let me analyze carefully what works and push harder on both exploitation and exploration.

**Champion structure:**
- S0: Explore(50) → S1 on BLACK(0.8), S1 on blue-visible(0.85), S1 on GRAY(0.6), S1 random(0.08)
- S1: Attract(2) → S0 on GRAY(0.9), S2 on few-neighbors(thresh=2,w=3), S0 on BLACK(0.1)
- S2: GoToBlue → S1 on BLACK(0.9), S0 random(0.05)

**Key observations:**
- The GRAY transition (0.6) in S0 sends robots to attract state even in middle - this helps aggregate
- Blue-visible detection helps robots find target area
- att=2 is optimal so far

**Final round strategy - maximize exploitation of champion topology with varied parameters:**
1. Try p0x2 (GRAY) = 0.4, 0.5, 0.65, 0.75, 0.8
2. Try p1x0 (GRAY escape) = 0.85, 0.95
3. Try att=1.5 approximated as att=2 (can only use integers? No, real in [1..5] - try 1.5)
4. Try w1x1=2, 4, 5 (neighbor sigmoid steepness)
5. Try adding blue-visible trigger to S1→S2
6. Try S0→S2 on GRAY instead of S1 (go find blue wall when in middle)
7. Try higher p0x0 (BLACK detection)

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
